完善稳定机制，深化投融资改革——2026 金融
街论坛年会

宏观 
报告日期 2026-10-22 专题报告 
  ⚫ 央行完善流动性支持。拟设非银机构大额赎回流动性支持
工具，扩大 SLF 适用范围，并推进利率框架改革，改善市
场稳定基础和利率传导。 
⚫ 证监会推进投融资改革。通过主动 ETF、长周期考核、公
募基金改革和回购增持改善投资条件，同时推进发行上
市、并购重组和再融资改革，支持企业发展。 
⚫ 风险治理与跨境便利化同步推进。金融监管总局加强机构
风险处置和科技金融服务，外汇局拟推出跨境结算、投融
资和汇率风险管理便利化措施。 
⚫ 股指持续修复仍需资金与盈利配合 。政策有助于稳定预
期、改善配置条件，后续重点关注权益资金持续流入，以
及盈利改善向更多行业和成分股扩散。

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

一、2026金融街论坛年会 
2026 金融街论坛年会于 10 月 19 日在北京金融街开幕，会期为 10 月 19 日至 22 日，由北
京市人民政府与中国人民银行、国家金融监督管理总局、中国证监会、新华通讯社、国家外
汇管理局共同主办，主题为“开放·合作·包容——共筑全球金融发展新生态” 。 
本届年会采用“主论坛 +平行论坛+专题大会+配套活动”架构，包含主论坛、平行论坛
及三场专题大会：金融科技大会聚焦智能经济；保险大会首次由金融监管总局主办，聚焦保
险业高质量发展，并拟发布相关研究成果；投资大会聚焦跨境投资与资本对接。年会首次将
分会场拓展至非洲，在肯尼亚内罗毕设置分会场，并安排北京、香港、伦敦三地连线。四部
门主要负责人出席开幕式并释放重要政策信号。论坛兼具政策发布、行业交流、国际合作和
投融资对接功能。 
二、会议解读 
本次论坛上，央行、金融监管总局、证监会、外汇局负责人先后发言，政策信号主要体
现为：完善金融市场稳定机制，同时改善资金参与资本市场的制度条件。前者重在防止流动
性冲击被放大，后者重在提高资金配置的稳定性和效率，两者有利于改善市场运行基础。 
1、央行行长潘功胜：完善流动性支持，提高利率传导效率 
央行提出设立应对非银机构大额赎回的流动性支持工具。当资管机构面临群体性流动性
压力时，产品集中赎回可能迫使机构出售债券，债券价格下跌又会压低产品净值，诱发更多
赎回，形成负反馈。新工具对此提供应对机制，旨在防止债券市场的流动性冲击扩大并向其
他市场传递。工具在特定压力情景下启用，操作须满足宏观审慎要求、提供高等级抵押品。
此外，扩大常备借贷便利（ SLF）适用范围，重在完善银行体系的临时流动性支持渠道；推
动银行间与交易所债券市场互联互通，有助于改善跨市场交易、流动性配置和价格形成。 
利率框架改革是另一条重要线索，涉及利率调控与贷款定价的“基准体系” 。具体包括三
个方面：一是研究择机将公开市场主要操作品种的期限由 7 天切换为隔夜，使公开市场隔夜
回购利率相应成为主要政策利率，提高利率调控的精准性；二是推动贷款定价基准多元化，
未来随着相关机制完善，财务能力强的企业可与金融机构协商，自主选择 DR001 或 LPR 作为

### Page 3

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

浮动利率贷款定价基准，逐步拓宽 LPR 以外的定价选择；三是推动原存贷款基准利率退出。
该利率自 2015 年 10 月以来长期未作调整，目前在存贷款定价中已不再发挥作用，下一步将
推动司法等少数领域的计息依据改用市场化利率指标。 
2、证监会主席吴清：完善稳市机制，推进投融资改革和市场治理 
吴清的讲话围绕市场稳定、包容性改革、市场生态和对外开放展开。投资端重点是健全
长周期考核机制、拓宽中长期资金入市渠道、深化公募基金改革，并宣布证监会当日将同意
首批 18 只主动 ETF 注册。同时，鼓励有条件的上市公司加大回购增持力度。这些安排分别作
用于机构考核、产品供给和股东回报，有助于提高权益配置的稳定性。 
融资端重点是落实“两创板”改革，推进发行上市、并购重组和再融资制度调整，提高
资本市场对科技企业和不同发展阶段企业的适应性。同时，支持证券公司并购重组及后续整
合，提升投资机构服务能力；推进北证 50ETF、北交所新股市值配售等安排，完善服务创新
型中小企业的市场体系。 
市场治理方面，筹建基金业运行监测中心、规范人工智能在证券期货业的应用、完善违
规减持处罚规则和先行赔付制度，分别加强风险监测、技术治理、交易约束和投资者保护。
政策着重提高监管有效性和市场规则的可预期性，为长期投资提供制度保障。 
对外开放方面，提升 QFII 投资便利度，支持境内外机构开发更多中国资产相关产品，推
进人民币股票交易柜台和 REITs 纳入沪深港通，拓宽跨境投资渠道。同时，强调防范跨境跨
市场风险传导，关注境外挂钩 A 股个股的杠杆产品等新风险点。 
3、金融监管总局局长丁向群、外汇局局长朱鹤新：推进风险治理、
机构转型和跨境便利化 
金融监管总局强调在风险治理基础上推进机构转型和资源配置调整。丁向群提出，通过
兼并重组、市场退出等方式稳妥处置高风险机构，强化早期纠正和穿透监管，减少风险积累
及其传染。同时，在保障基础服务、考虑承载能力和保持适度竞争的条件下，因地制宜推进
中小机构减量提质，推动机构形成与自身能力相匹配的业务定位。 
金融服务结构调整同样重要。银行资金要更好适应先进制造业、服务业和基础设施建设
需要，保险则要增强灾害、航运、养老健康和科技创新等领域的保障能力。支持商业航天、

### Page 4

交易咨询业务资格：证监许可【2012】31 号

生物医药、智能机器人等领域专业共保体建设，说明科技金融不仅涉及提供融资，也涉及分
担研发和产业化风险。相关业务能否形成稳定盈利，取决于金融机构的专业服务、风险定价
和资本管理能力。 
外汇局重在提高企业跨境经营便利度，完善双向资金配置条件。朱鹤新介绍，拟推出的
增量措施涉及经常项目便利化、资本项目外汇管理优化，以及外汇市场开放和证券公司结售
汇业务试点。政策直接服务于企业跨境结算、投融资和汇率风险管理，重在降低业务办理成
本，提高资金配置效率。 
这一方向同时服务“引进来”和“走出去” 。结合央行关于人民币清算、离岸市场和流动
性供给的部署，相关政策有助于改善企业跨境经营和人民币资产配置的基础条件。对 A 股的
影响主要通过经营成本、融资条件和投资便利度传导。外资是否增加权益配置，仍取决于盈
利、估值、汇率和全球资产的相对吸引力。 
3、股指启发 
本届论坛的政策意义，主要在于稳定市场预期、改善长期投资条件，并支持企业融资与
经营。稳市机制和流动性支持，有助于减少集中赎回、被动抛售和跨市场风险传染，改善投
资者持有权益资产的信心；主动 ETF、投资便利化和长周期考核，有助于丰富配置渠道、减
少短期业绩压力对投资行为的干扰，回购增持则通过实际执行形成购买需求。这些政策对股
指的支持，需逐步落实为持续配置和盈利改善。 
对股指而言，长期投资条件改善，有利于盈利稳定、股东回报较好的企业获得配置支持 。
融资改革和科技金融则为相关企业研发、产业化和盈利增长提供条件。政策效果将通过资金
配置与企业经营逐步体现 。若盈利改善扩散至更多行业和成分股，并伴随持续资金流入，指
数修复基础将更稳固。若经营兑现偏慢或风险偏好回落，市场仍可能反复。

分析师介绍：

和宏观研究。曾获期货日报"最佳宏观金融期货分析师"称号。 
杨弘毅：国贸期货研究院宏观金融研究助理，厦门大学财务学博士。专注于宏观经济、
股指与金融衍生品研究，擅长将机器学习、人工智能等量化技术应用于股指及金融衍生品研
究领域，构建套利、趋势跟踪等交易策略。

子公司 AeternZ 於 當 地 建 置 產 能 ， 但 現 階 段 仍 未 揭 露 合 作 金 額。 NVIDIA 於 07/2026
與 Amkor (AMKR US) 也 簽 約 相 似 的 預 付 款 合 約。顯 示 AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片
(SoC) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展 ，封 測 複 雜 度 提 升 ，IC 設 計 廠 和 封 測 廠 合 作 深 化，
提 高 後 段 封 測 廠 訂 單 能 見 度 。 不 過 和NVIDIA 合作的此協議短期效益有限，京元電評
價合理 。

當 地 建 置 產 能，但 現 階 段 仍 未 揭 露 合 作 金 額：  
10/13/2026 京 元 電 重 訊 公 告 ， 董 事 會 決 議 通 過 與NVIDIA(NVDA US) 簽 訂 客
戶 預 付 款 協 議，將 由 NVIDIA 預 先 支 付 款 項，支 持 京 元 電 及 美 國 全 資 子 公 司
AeternZ Semiconductor Testing Corporation 在 美 國 建 置 測 試 產 能 ， 未 來 再 以
提 供 測 試 服 務 抵 減 預 付 款。京 元 電 同 步 宣 布，因 合 約 架 構 調 整，取 消 原 先 對
美 國 子 公 司 提 供 的 3 億 美 元 背 書 保 證 額 度 。 
京 元 電 此 次簽約 對 象 為 NVIDIA International, Inc. ， NVIDIA 預 付 款 項 ， 京
元 電 及 其 美 國 全 資 子 公 司 將 在 美 國 建 置 測 試 產 能 ， 為 NVIDIA 保 留 產 能 並
提 供 測 試 服 務，預 付 款 則 以 未 來 服 務 款 項 抵 減。此 合 作 並 非 股 權 投 資 或 合 資
模 式，而 是 在 既 有 客 戶 關 係 下，透 過 客 戶 提 前 提 供 資 金，支 持 供 應 商 擴 充 產
能 並 確 保 未 來 測 試 資 源 。 
京 元 電 董 事 會 已 於 07/ 2026 決 議 通 過 美 國 投 資 設 廠 案，規 劃 在 美 國 設 立 孫 公
司 並 興 建 新 廠 ， 投 資 金 額 以 14 億美元 (約 新 台 幣 450 億元 )為 上 限 。 公 司 當
時 表 示，此 投 資 主 要 因 應 未 來 營 運 需 求 及 全 球 供 應 鏈 布 局，後 續 將 依 設 廠 進
度 與 資 金 需 求 逐 步 投 入 ， 資 金 來 源 以 自 有 資 金 或 借 款 等 方 式 支 應 。 
此 次 協 議 有 助 於 提 高 新 增 產 能 的 需 求 能 見 度，並 降 低 部 分 初 期 資 金 壓 力。不
過，由 於 預 付 款 金 額、實 際 保 留 產 能 規 模 及 相 關 訂 單 金 額 尚 未 公 開，現 階 段
仍 無 法 量 化 對 營 收 與 獲 利 的 貢 獻；短 期 營 運 表 現 仍 取 決 於 既 有 產 能 擴 充、新
晶 片 測 試 需 求 及 美 國 廠 建 置 進 度 。

元 ：

服務 。

供 服 務 逐 步 抵 減 ， 依 實 際 生 產 規 模 及 服 務 提 供 速 度 ， 預 估 合 作 期 間 約5~10

2 群益投顧 
測 試。兩 者 都 能 提 高 產 能 需 求 能 見 度，但 實 際 營 收 與 獲 利 貢 獻 仍 取 決 於 產 能
開 出 、 訂 單 規 模 及 服 務 條 件 。 
外 媒 報 導 京 元 電 美 國 子 公 司預 計 落 腳 德 州 沃 斯 堡 市(Fort Worth )：  
在 美 國 設 廠 地 點 方 面， 外 媒 於10/ 2026 報 導， 京 元 電 全 資 美 國 子 公 司AeternZ
正 評 估 於 德 州 沃 斯 堡(Fort Worth )設 立 半 導 體 測 試 廠，初 步 規 劃 投 資 約 13 億
美 元 ， 其 中 約 10 億 美 元 用 於 不 動 產 、3 億 美 元 用 於 設 備 ， 選 址 涉 及 West 
Everman Parkway 一 處 約 23 英 畝 的 土 地 。 沃 斯 堡 市 議 會 預 計 於 10 /27/2026
審 議 相 關 租 稅 優 惠，但 目 前 尚 不 能 視 為 京 元 電 已 正 式 決 定 設 廠 地 點，仍 須 觀
察 後 續 選 址 及 投 資 進 度。若 該 地 點 獲 選，德州相對 台 積 電 所 在 的 亞 利 桑 那 州
鳳 凰 城 聚 落 ，地 緣 上 相 對 鄰 近 AI 伺 服 器 供 應 鏈 ，代 表 AI 晶 片 完 成 測 試 後，
可 就 近 送 往 當 地 代 工 廠 ， 進 行 整 機 組 裝 與 系 統 測 試 。不 過 ， 廠 房 建 置 、 設 備
進 駐 及 客 戶 認 證 仍 需 時 間 ， 實 際 貢 獻 時 點 取 決 於 建 廠 進 度 與 測 試 需 求 。 
AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片(SoC) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展， 封 測 複 雜
度 提 升 ， IC 設 計 廠 和 封 測 廠 合 作 深 化：  
隨著 AI 晶 片 由 傳 統 單 一 系 統 單 晶 片(SoC ) 朝 Chiplet 及 異 質 整 合 發 展 ， 半 導
體 產 業 的 競 爭 已 不 再 侷 限 於 晶 片 設 計 與 先 進 製 程，也 延 伸 至 先 進 封 裝、晶 粒
間 高 速 互 連、散 熱 及 測 試 驗 證 等 環 節。晶 片 架 構 複 雜 度 提 高，使 封 裝 與 測 試
成 為 影 響 產 品 效 能 、 成 本 及 量 產 能 力 的 重 要 因 素 。 
對 京 元 電 而 言，若 能 持 續 參 與 NVIDIA 新 晶 片 的 測 試 開 發 與 驗 證，並 承 接 後
續 量 產 測 試，有 助 於 深 化 客 戶 關 係 及 鞏 固 高 階 測 試 業 務 的 競 爭 優 勢；但 此 次
預付款協議本身並未改變雙方的股權關係，合作價值仍須透過後續訂單與
產 能 利 用 率 驗 證 。 
相 較 之 下 ， 力 成(6239 TT) 與 Broadcom (AVGO US) 規 劃 在 新 加 坡 成 立 先 進 封
裝 合 資 公 司 ， 發 展 扇 出 型 面 板 級 封 裝 （FOPLP ）及 重 布 線 層 （RDL ） 等 相 關
技術，屬於 IC 設 計 公 司 直 接 參 與 封 裝 製 造 平 台 投 資 的 模 式 。 此 合 作 不 僅 涉
及 產 能 配 置，也 涵 蓋 新 技 術 開 發 與 量 產 導 入，反 映 當 先 進 封 裝 成 為 影 響 晶 片
效 能 、 互 連 密 度 及 量 產 成 本 的 關 鍵 環 節 ，IC 設 計 公 司 可 能 更 積 極 參 與 封 裝
技 術 與 產 能 布 局。對 力 成 而 言，若 能 順 利 完 成 製 程 驗 證、良 率 提 升 及 量 產 導
入，有 機 會 拓 展 新 的 技 術 平 台 與 業 務 成 長 機 會；惟 仍 須 觀 察 客 戶 訂 單、量 產
進 度 、 資 本 支 出 及 投 資 報 酬 率 。 
整 體 而 言 ，NVIDIA 與 京 元 電 、Amkor 的 合 作 ， 主 要 是 透 過 客 戶 預 付 款 支 持
產 能 建 置 ， 強 化 供 應 商 的 需 求 與 資 金 能 見 度 ；Broadcom 與 力 成 的 合 作 ， 則
是 透 過 共 同 投 資 發 展 新 的 先 進 封 裝 技 術 平 台 。 兩 者 反 映AI 半 導 體 產 業 的 合
作 模 式 正 逐 步 深 化，但 資 金 安 排、技 術 參 與 程 度 及 投 資 風 險 並 不 相 同。投 資
上，京 元 電 應 關 注 預 付 款 規 模、美 國 新 廠 投 產 及 高 階 測 試 訂 單 成 長；力 成 則
應 觀 察 先 進 封 裝 技 術 商 業 化、量 產 良 率 與 合 資 事 業 的 投 資 報 酬。京 元 電 此 次
協 議 對 中 長 期 業 務 發 展 具 正 面 意 義，但 在 實 際 財 務 貢 獻 尚 未 明 朗 前，宜 視 為
客 戶 關 係 深 化 及 產 能 布 局 的 正 面 訊 號，而 非 直 接 上 修 獲 利 預 估 的 依 據。由於
目 前 京 元 電 評 價 合 理 ，投 資 建 議 Neutral 。

4 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

樺漢 (6414 TT)

永豐觀點 
三大事業體持續成長，第三季營收同期新高，未來營運將聚焦於 Physical AI 市場
發展。 
 投資評價與建議 
維持買進投資建議：主係考量(1) 實體 AI 應用落地，包含 AMR、自動駕駛、智慧
工廠、機器人等應用，樺漢從產品、平台、軟硬整合延伸，潛在市場成長空間相當
大；(2) Kontron 完成整併，策略整合綜效逐漸顯現，擴大競爭優勢；(3) 除三大
事業體接單動能穩健外， 智能零售市場成長性高，樺漢將從硬體製造跨足到軟體平
台 ， 目前合作對象皆外國內外知名餐飲及零售商等 。 評價方面 ， 以2027 年稅後 EPS 
28.5 元，目前本益比約 14 倍，過往歷史本益比區間為 13~22 倍，考量在手訂單
持續墊高，營運規模效益可期，故維持買進的投資建議，目標價485 元(17X 2027 
EPS)。

ESG 評析 
樺漢企業永續評鑑整體為 B+等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 B 等與 A 等。

樺漢為全球工業電腦大廠：樺漢科技股份有限公司成立於 1999 年，為鴻海轉投資
工業電腦廠，產品包括 POS(point of sale)系統、ATM 系統、工業控制系統、車載
系統 、 人機互動式產品和其他周邊如處理器 、 記憶體等產品 。 最大股東為鴻海集團 ，
持有 24.1%股權(包括寶鑫國際 22.7%、鴻揚創投 1.4%)。樺漢主要分成三大事業
群：工業物聯網、智慧軟體與方案、智慧工廠與廠務。2026 年 1-9 月營收占比：
工業物聯網 45.7%、智慧工廠與廠務 42.6％、智慧軟體與方案 11.7%。 
邊緣運算及半導體應用， 3Q26 營收單季新高 ：樺漢 3Q26 營收 496.4 億元
(+2.6%QoQ，+47.4%YoY)， 成長主係來自於工業物聯網中邊緣運算需求強勁以及
智慧工廠受惠於半導體客戶廠務工程進度持續推薦 ， 智慧軟體與方案部門因整併及
專 案 進 度 影 響 略 微 衰 退， 研 究 部 預 估 在 產 品 組 合 差 異 不 大 下 ， 毛 利 率
19.5%(+0.2pts QoQ，-2.2ptsYoY)，營利率 5.9%(+0.3ptsQoQ，+2.1ptsYoY)，
稅後淨利 10.35 億元(-1.5%QoQ，+8.9%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 6.59 元。 
訂單能見度佳，2026 年高成長不變：展望第四季，樺漢維持全年高成長目標，三
大事業體出貨狀況良好，其中 NCRV 全年預估貢獻 160 億元，Kontron 亦完成收
購並持續深化營運綜效，智慧工廠業務訂單將逐季認列營收。研究部預估樺漢
4Q26 營收 519.9 億元(+4.7%QoQ，+31.6%YoY)，毛利率 19.6%(+0.1ptsQoQ，
-1ptsYoY)，營利率 6.7%(+0.8ptsQoQ，-1.2ptsYoY)，稅後淨利 11.37 億元
(+9.8%QoQ，+30.6%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 7.24 元。預估 2026 年營收 1871.3 億
元 (+31.5%YoY)， 受 產 品 組 合 影 響 ， 毛 利 率19.6%(-0.9pts YoY) ， 營 利 率
6%(+1.4pts YoY)，稅後淨利 39.75 億元(+23.7%YoY)，稅後 EPS 為 25.32 元。 
在手訂單 2550 億元，朝向成為高毛利軟硬整合系統商：樺漢集團策略朝向聚焦高
毛利高成長事業，如從 Physical AI 相關產業進行全面整合，以成為系統整合商為
營運重點，目前在手訂單維持 2550 億高檔並持續推升。此外，持續整合Kontron
營運創造縱效，透過技術整合及市場聚焦，管理層目標 Kontron2030 年為營收達
26 億歐元、EBITDA 目標 4.2 億歐元，並透過持續降低供應鏈採購成本及製造成
本，擴大營運槓桿優勢，提高軟體等高價值產品。獲利方面，預估高毛利產品營收
占比將持續提升，產業聚焦於零售餐飲、智慧能源與樓宇、交通、醫療長照、智慧
城市與公共安全、智慧製造等。展望未來，預估 2027 年 NCRV 營收年增 15%，
智慧零售將成為下一個成長動能 ，樺漢將打造平台的生台戲 ， 從方案代理商製產品
銷售並與 Nvidia 及聯發科技術合作。長期來看，無論是透過策略合作或者併購，

營運現況與分析

樺漢在供應鏈、生產和管理成本上都將達到合作的綜效；智慧工廠與廠務(帆宣)方

體營收，優化獲利能力。另一方面，實體 AI 市場持續擴大中，樺漢已有許多專案

Document 206: [329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

00

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 
기다렸던 4 행정 중속 엔진 캐파 증설 단행 
HD현대중공업의 4행정 엔진 캐파는 기존 3.0GW에서 증설이 완료되는 '28년부터 
램프업을 거쳐 '30년 7.2GW까지 확대될 계획이다. ①[육상 발전용]은 신공장(울산 
온산) 3GW 와 HD 현대엔진(전남 영암) 1GW 를 합쳐 4.0GW, ②[선박용]은 
2.3GW에서 3.2GW로 확대된다. HD현대엔진 캐파는 기존 0.7GW에서 1GW까
지 확대되며, 해당 라인은 신공장이 가동되기 전 ‘27~’28 년 구간에서의 선박&육상 엔
진 모두 대응 가능하다. 신공장 캐파는 총 3.0GW로, ‘28년 약 30%의 가동률로 시
작해 램프업을 거쳐 ‘30 년부터 풀캐파로 20MW급 모델을 생산할 예정이다. 4 행정 
중속엔진에 대한 수요 가시성은 높다. 동사가 공유했던 육상 발전 엔진 인콰이어리 규
모는 기존 약 3~9GW였으나, 현재는 이를 크게 상회하는 수준으로 소통하고 있다.

최근 부유식 데이터센터(FDC)에 대한 하이퍼스케일러들의 인콰이어리 또한 확대되고 
있다. HD 한국조선해양은 최근 미국 선급(ABS)로부터 100MW급 규모의 부유식 발
전 설비(BMPP)에 대한 기본 인증(AiP)를 획득했으며, 동사는 FDC 신조 사업과 더불
어 발전원 4 행정 엔진을 파워십 및 부유식 발전설비(BMPP)에 납품하며 그룹사들과 
패키지 사업으로 진행할 예정이다. 가시화된 수요를 기반으로 엔진기계사업부의 구조적 
성장 보여줄 것으로 전망하며, 이에 따른 리레이팅을 예상한다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원 유지 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원을 유지한다. 동사는 
올해 상선수주 목표 이미 초과달성하며 안정적인 펀더멘털 유지 중이다. 하반기 남은 
기간은 LNGC, VLGC/VLAC 중심의 선별 수주 전략을 통해 ‘28 년 이후의 상선 부
문 수익성 유지 충분히 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 글로벌 지정학적 리스크 확대에 따른 
해군 현대화 사이클 기조에 맞추어 미 해군은 동맹국의 호위함 구매를 고려하고 있으
며, MASGA를 기반으로 중장기적으로 미국 전투함을 건조하기 위해 HD현대의 미국 
진출 가능성 높다는 판단이다. 또한 미국을 제외한 글로벌 함정(페루, 필리핀, 말레이시
아, 칠레, 사우디, 이집트 등) 수주 파이프라인도 다수 보유하고 있어 함정 모멘텀에 따
른 리레이팅 충분히 가능할 전망이다. 특수선+4 행정 엔진+FDC+SMR 등 조선소에 
붙일 수 있는 모든 추가 성장동력을 보유한 유일한 종목이기에 조선업 최선호주(Top-
Pick)로 제시한다. 최근 불거지고 있는 노조 파업 문제 해소될 시 저점 매수 기회라는 
판단이다.

2026-09-17

조선/방산. 한승한, R.A 고서영 / shane.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-9992

HD현대중공업 수정TP(원)

Document 228: AI融资担忧重创科技股，纳指跌超2%，10年期美债收益率跌破5%，黄金重挫，原油大涨

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI融资担忧重创科技股，纳指跌超2%，10年期美债收益率跌破5%，黄金重挫，原油大涨.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

芯⽚指数跌 6.72% 。 CoreWeave 跌 10% 。 Meta 跌 7% 。美光科技下跌 6.26% 。西部数据下
跌 6.43% ，盘后绩后下挫超 5% 。光通信 Coherent 跌超 12% 。 2 年期⾄ 10 年期美债收益率降
超 15 个基点。法德利差⼀度扩⼤⾄ 175 个基点、创 2011 年欧债危机以来最⾼。⻩⾦跌近
3% ，创 8 ⽉ 3 ⽇以来新低。⽩银下跌 6% 。布油涨 4% 。⽐特币跌 3% 。
摘要：
Meta 财报引爆 AI 资本开⽀恐慌，美股科技板块重挫，避险情绪推动美债⼤涨，原油则在中东局
势升温下飙升。
Meta三季度营收超出预期，却因资本开⽀继续上调、未来⽀出承诺升⾄ 8250 亿美元⽽令⾃由现
⾦流转负，五年期信⽤违约互换报价创下有记录以来新⾼。融资担忧沿着 AI 产业链扩散。
周四标普 500 指数收于 7662.54 点，跌 1.22% ，终结三连涨；纳斯达克综合指数跌 2.60% ，道琼
斯⼯业平均指数涨 0.02% ，罗素 2000 指数涨 0.11% ，费城半导体指数跌 6.72% 。板块上，美股⽇

⽤消费品、医疗保健与公⽤事业领涨，半导体、存储芯⽚与光通信领跌。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

恐慌情绪迅速蔓延⾄利率市场，美国国债遭到抢购，10 年期美债收益率单⽇暴跌逾 15 个基点，
⼀度跌破 5.00% ，创⼀个⽉新低。法国政局动荡引发的欧洲债市危机，以及针对 CTA 空头的⼤规
模逼空，共同放⼤了此次美债涨幅。
沙特能源设施遇袭，美国与以⾊列被曝已经制定在中期选举后打击伊朗的⽅案，布伦特原油被
推到五个⽉⾼位，WTI 原油升⾄五个⽉⾼点，每桶逼近 100 美元。美元⼩幅收低，⻩⾦与⽐特币
均遭抛售。
AI 融资链条裂痕扩散， Meta ⽀出承诺升⾄ 8250 亿美元
周四标普 500 指数下跌 1.5% ，⽽等权重标普 500 指数（衡量市场⼴度的指标）相对于标普 500 指
数则创下⾃ 2024 年 7 ⽉以来的最佳单⽇表现。
抛售集中于科技巨头，科技七巨头表现远逊于标普⼤盘。亚⻢逊下跌 4.57% ，英伟达下跌
4.40% ，⾕歌下跌 3.15% 。

### Page 3

华尔街⻅闻提及，公司把未来租赁与合同⽀出承诺，从 7 ⽉披露的近 7000 亿美元上调⾄约 8250
亿美元，其中⼤部分指向 AI 算⼒与基础设施。这笔钱覆盖的是多年期付款承诺，规模远超单年度
资本开⽀。
投资者的关注点随即转向融资端。美国洲际交易所数据显⽰， Meta 五年期信⽤违约互换报价盘
中⼀度上⾏ 8.9 个基点⾄ 119.3 个基点，创该合约开始交易以来的盘中新⾼，公司债相对美国国债
同步⾛弱。
因此尽管 Meta 三季度营收与利润均超预期，股价仍下跌 6.98% 。

### Page 4

CoreWeave 是最直接的参照。这家新云服务商股价下跌 10.06% ，其 2030 年到期、票息 9% 的债
券收益率回到 15% 上⽅，信⽤违约互换报价维持在 1000 个基点以上。市场对 AI 基础设施的债务
定价，已经开始脱离股权定价。
半导体设备与光通信领跌，存储股回撤继续扩⼤
泛林集团下跌 11.76% ，是当天跌幅最⼤的⼤型科技股之⼀。公司给出的指引不及市场预期，供
应链限制让投资者担⼼设备企业的增⻓兑现节奏。科磊下跌 9.55% ，应⽤材料下跌 8.16% ，半导
体设备环节同步承压。
光通信板块跌幅更深。 Coherent 下跌 12.51% ， Lumentum 下跌 11.54% 。数据中⼼建设节奏⼀旦
放缓，这两家公司所在的环节会最先被削减订单。
光通信、 Agentic AI 与 AI 数据中⼼跌幅居前，标普 500 剔除 AI 赋能股后仍录得上涨。

### Page 5

存储芯⽚同样未能幸免。美光科技下跌 6.26% ，希捷科技下跌 7.86% 。西部数据下跌 6.43% ，盘
后绩后继续下挫超 5% 。
研究机构 Bespoke 的统计显⽰，存储股⾃⾼点的回撤幅度已达 41.6% ，半导体⾏业 ETF ⾃ 10 ⽉ 5
⽇以来累计下跌 16% ，距 6 ⽉的历史⾼点回落 20.3% 。
英伟达 CEO ⻩仁勋当天表⽰，市场对新云服务商的需求⾼得超乎想象，但资本供应不⾜正在制
约⾏业增⻓。富国银⾏交易员在报告中提到，这番话让市场感到不安，再度引发投资者对信贷
市场承接能⼒的担忧。
巴克莱交易团队认为，半导体股近期的反弹更像⼋连跌之后的正常均值回归。 AI 板块整体参与度
偏弱，尤其在新云与存储两个⽅向，说明投资者并未⼴泛重回这笔交易，仍在密切跟踪信⽤市
场。

### Page 6

资⾦转向防御板块，⼴度与价格出现罕⻅背离
资⾦转向防御板块。宝洁上涨 5.58% ，好时上涨 4.67% ，家得宝上涨 4.33% ，强⽣上涨 3.81% ，
可⼝可乐上涨 2.88% ，⻨当劳上涨 2.59% ，耐克上涨 2.10% 。
苹果上涨 1.23% ，是七巨头中唯⼀收涨的⼀只。充沛的⾃由现⾦流与相对克制的基础设施投⼊，
让它在这⼀轮资⾦再分配中获得了买盘。
华尔街⻅闻提及，⿊⾊素瘤疫苗数据提前泄露，结果不及预期。 Moderna ⼀度⼤跌 26% 。

### Page 7

FBB Capital Partners 研究总监 Michael Bailey 表⽰，当天市场给出的是今年已多次出现的轮
动，只是更为剧烈。 Meta 的业绩让空头占得上⻛，触发投资者抛售近期的 AI 赢家，转向医疗保
健、消费与公⽤事业这类防御板块。
彭博宏观策略师 Michael Ball 认为，随着融资成本上升，⾃由现⾦流会成为区分科技股更重要的
指标。 Alphabet 、 SpaceX 、 Anthropic 与 OpenAI 正在同时争夺巨额资本，⽽信⽤投资者对项⽬
回报变得更为挑剔，边际 AI 项⽬需要给出更清晰的回报路径。
科技股回撤托底美债， 10 年期收益率跌破 5% ，美元⼩幅⾛低
欧洲债券危机带来的避险资⾦流⼊，叠加市场对美股⼈⼯智能增⻓前景的担忧，美债周四⼤
涨。
10 年期美债收益率收于 4.95% ，全天下降约 19 个基点，为 9 ⽉ 2 ⽇以来⾸次跌破 5% ，也是近⼀个
⽉最低⽔平。
5 年期收益率下降约 21 个基点⾄ 4.76% ， 30 年期下降约 15 个基点⾄ 5.29% ，中短端跑赢⻓端。

### Page 8

华尔街⻅闻提及，随着多家科技巨头财报引发市场对 AI 资本⽀出可持续性的质疑，美债收益率全
线⼤幅回落。⾼盛 Rich Privorotsky 认为，AI 资本⽀出的每⼀分回撤，都意味着潜在债券供给的
减少，从⽽直接为债市减压。
在 Privorotsky 看来，当超⼤规模云计算企业发⾏债务的规模已可与美国国债相提并论时， AI 资
本⽀出的任何收缩，实质上等同于减少债券市场的潜在供给压⼒，其效果近似于⼀次量化宽
松。
法国主权债务危机的持续发酵也为美债提供了额外⽀撑。法国 10 年期国债收益率升⾄ 5.076% ，
为 2002 年 6 ⽉以来最⾼。法德利差⼀度扩⼤⾄ 175 个基点、创 2011 年欧债危机以来最⾼。

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Recent revisions to US PCE inflation, UK productivity
and Canadian population estimates are reminders that
statistical measurement is imperfect. Revisions are both a
fact of life and, when they improve measurement, desirable.
• Australian data will also be revised over time, though

not necessarily in the same ways as these latest revisions
abroad. The ABS tends to favour quality over speed,
which reduces the need for early revisions as more data
become available. But revisions can still matter. Population
estimates implied in the initial releases of labour market and
GDP data are especially likely to be revised in the near term.
• Estimates of current trends can change as future data are
published. Shifting seasonal patterns are another source

of revision, especially for monthly CPI components with
short histories. Series that are derived from multiple other
imperfectly measured series are more prone to revision
than quantities that can be measured directly. All these
considerations point to a need for humility when using
recent data to make strong policy pronouncements.
On September 30, the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation,
PCE, was revised down. New methods and data sources were
applied to three components of services inflation (financial

advice, software and legal services), large enough to result in
downward revisions to the overall read on core PCE inflation.
These changes had been pre-announced in June and were
therefore not a surprise. The CPI measure of inflation was not
affected, nor should the revisions materially change your view
of US inflationary pressures.
Some revisions are more consequential for interpretation and
policy. A couple of weeks before the US PCE revision, it was

revealed that the UK Office for National Statistics was revising
down its estimates of average hours worked per worker. The
result was that measured productivity growth was nowhere
near as weak as had been widely believed. As my friend and
counterpart, TS Lombard’s Chief Economist Freya Beamish
commented on social media, “Oh well. Not like any major policy
decisions were taken on the basis of those numbers.” Quite.
Also in September, Statistics Canada revised up its estimates

of Canada’s population, taking the recent growth rates
from outright contraction to still-slow growth a little below
½%yr. The number of temporary residents in Canada is still
falling, but the revised data do a better job of tracking them,
especially those seeking an extension on their permit. (An alert
When reality gets revised
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
for customers who have seen the cross-country population
graph I often use at client events: the Canadian line now looks

different but still shows a sharp slowing.)
There is no fault to be attributed here. Revisions to key
statistics are a part of economic life. Sometimes the underlying
data arrive with a lag, perhaps because of reporting deadlines.
Sometimes the compiling agency improves its methods or
gains access to a better data source. The US and UK examples
above fall into this category. In the Canadian case, better
data and a more accurate definition of scope were involved.

All these revisions should be welcomed. They provide a more
accurate picture of the underlying reality.
Australia, revised
Revisions are a fact of life for Australian statistics, too. I am
still a little scarred by that unexpected –0.5%qtr GDP print
when the September quarter 2016 data were first released on
my first day as RBA Assistant Governor; the figure has since
been revised to 0%qtr and was followed by a big bounce-back
in the December quarter.

The recent revisions mentioned above are less likely to be
replicated in Australia, though. Australia’s CPI is designed such
that the headline (not seasonally adjusted) measure is never
revised. This is because it is used in many legal contracts.
If an error is detected, the ABS makes the adjustment in
subsequent months and quarters rather than revising the
history. Our labour force survey has a much higher response
rate than its UK equivalent – Australians are a compliant lot

when the ABS comes knocking – and measures actual hours
worked more reliably. And our population statistics already
handle the array of temporary resident visas with the Bureau
instead applying a standard 12-month-in-16-month residency
test to count as a resident for the population statistics rather
than using a visa-classification basis.
This last case illustrates Australia’s broader tendency to
favour quality over speed in official statistics. Our CPI and

national accounts data tend to be published a bit later than
peer countries’ data for the same reference period, and our
“Economic data are often revised. Sometimes
the revisions change the story; sometimes
they do not."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
estimated resident population data is quite a bit lagged
relative to Canada’s (the 12-month-in-16 criteria taking over
a year to confirm). The trade-off is that observers of the
Australian economy must wait longer but can be less worried
about “flash” and “advance” releases of data that can be

quickly revised to tell a completely different story.
Revisions to data still can matter in Australia, and it helps
to be aware of the specific cases where it might occur. For
example, precisely because the population data are very
lagged, the population estimates underlying the initial
releases of the labour force survey and national accounts are
based on preliminary data and get revised later as a matter
of routine. This is why ratios such as the unemployment,

employment-to-population and participation rates provide a
better real-time gauge of labour market conditions than the
monthly change in employment. Similarly, GDP per capita can
be revised even when GDP is not. This type of revision is also
a more likely source of short-term changes in measured hours
worked in the Australian productivity data than the conceptual
change that shifted the UK data.
The usual reporting lags – and occasional correction of

reporting errors by the reporting entity – will always be a factor
in Australia just as it is in other countries. The annual national
accounts, due on 23 October, incorporate additional data, revise
the quarterly estimates and provide first estimates of important
quantities such as the capital stock. Just as importantly, though,
the annual accounts reconcile the different ways of measuring
GDP. In doing so, what we thought we knew about the recent

history of output growth can change.
Every purchase is also a sale, so conceptually, you should get the
same answer for final output regardless of whether you count
how much is produced, how much people spent on it, or how
much income people got for producing it. In practice, though,
the “production”, “expenditure” and “income” approaches do
not always line up until the ABS goes through a more thorough
reconciliation process in compiling the annual accounts.

When the future changes the past
Future data can also change our view of the past. Two good
examples are trend estimation and seasonal adjustment. Future
observations help reveal whether an apparent trend is stable.
As a result, real-time trend estimates can differ considerably
from those calculated with the benefit of later data.
Identifying stable seasonal patterns also requires a reasonable
run of data. Future observations affect whether an earlier

movement is judged to be normal seasonal variation. This
problem is more pronounced for newer series, where there is
not yet enough history to establish what “normal” looks like.
Consider, then, the monthly CPI. This relatively new measure has
only a short run of data with many individual components having
too little history to use standard seasonal adjustment methods.
As Westpac’s senior economist Justin Smirk has previously
highlighted, some components such as footwear are being

‘seasonally adjusted’ by applying Henderson trends, similar
to those used in the ‘trend’ measures of labour market and
national accounts data. For some other components, including
books, furniture, audio-visual and computing equipment and
children’s clothes, certain price falls – usually associated with
sales periods – are just arbitrarily removed from the seasonally
adjusted series. The index is held at its previous month’s level
but otherwise matches the non-seasonally adjusted series.

The seasonally adjusted measures of these CPI components will
inevitably be revised over time, and thus so could the monthly
trimmed mean estimates. This helps explain why the RBA has
said it would, for now, focus on the quarterly trimmed mean
based on the old seasonal factors. That measure will at least
not be buffeted by shifting measurement of seasonality in a
short history. If seasonal patterns are themselves also shifting,
the current underlying trend might look a bit different in a few

years, not just a smoother month-to-month profile.
The broader point here is to remember that economic statistics
are a lens on reality. Sometimes the lens is smudged, sometimes
the scope is off and sometimes what is being read as underlying
trends is wrong. Measures constructed from several imperfect
series – productivity from output and hours worked, or unit labour
costs from productivity and labour costs, or savings rates derived
from income and spending – are especially prone to revision

compared with concepts that can be measured more directly, like
a price. That is a strong argument for humility before making firm
policy pronouncements based on simple data comparisons.

THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
consistent with a labour market that is gradually normalising
than one entering a meaningful downturn.
The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish
message, with participants noting they had "not seen

sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and
expressing concern that inflation risks remained "skewed to the
upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing
persistent goods price strength to the effects of the AI buildout
despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was
also concern that, after more than five years of inflation above
target, elevated inflation could begin to influence inflation

expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In
contrast, labour market conditions were generally viewed as
stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority
noted the labour market had strengthened somewhat recently,
several participants highlighted unusually low dynamism,
characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding
rate and elevated long-term unemployment. Importantly,
participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth

was moderate and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2
percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor market
was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".
While participants broadly expected AI-related investment to
boost productivity growth and potential output over time, several
warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand
to outpace aggregate supply over the medium term, putting
upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some

participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate
and several judged the current policy stance to be "not restrictive
or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants
supported the September rate increase, and most assessed
that another increase in the federal funds rate would likely be
appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain
data dependent.
The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted

a similar picture of an economy that remains resilient but is
gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9
from 55.4 in August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in
business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export orders, which
slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight
The only data release of note in Australia this week was
the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Survey ,
which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At

80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to deeply pessimistic
levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock
when readings were among the weakest in the survey’s
more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest
rate pressures are once again clearly front of mind for
consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a
year ago’ and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0%
and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the RBA’s

September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact –
sentiment tanked 20% between those surveyed before the
RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.
Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient,
but they still weakened, with the one-year and five-year ahead
sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers
are becoming more ‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not
outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively

low versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high
of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip demand. We
expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force
Survey – see our preview for more detail. All told, with higher
interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening
jobs market all weighing at once, households are entering the
final months of the year on the back foot.
Offshore, the focus was on US data.
Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that

September non-farm payrolls came in softer than expected
across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k
expected), and there were sizeable downward revisions
to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July
and August payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k
respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure
& hospitality and education & health services industries,
although construction and manufacturing employment
continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related

investment. The unemployment rate edged up to 4.2%, with
softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force
participation to 61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated
to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average
pace of job creation slowing to around 50k. However, softer
payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market
deterioration. Lower immigration and ageing demographics

have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist
“A key focus was the interaction between
AI investment, productive capacity and
inflation."

months. Labour market conditions improved modestly with
the employment index moving to broadly neutral territory
50.1 after two months below 50, supported by elevated

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Fuel, housing and the bumpy road ahead
Higher fuel costs, a weaker exchange rate, and a softer
housing market have prompted a refresh of our near-term
forecasts. Inflation looks set to finish 2026 above 4%, while
growth could lose a little momentum into year-end. Even so,

we continue to expect the RBNZ to pause in October before
lifting the OCR to 3% in December.
New Zealand’s recovery is still moving forward, but the road
has become rougher. Since our last forecast update in August,
global oil and refined-fuel prices have climbed sharply, the
New Zealand dollar has fallen, and longer-term interest rates
have moved higher. At home, the housing market has remained
weaker than we anticipated. None of those developments,

on their own, are enough to derail the recovery. Together,
however, they point to a tougher finish to 2026 for households
and businesses. We have therefore revisited our near-term
inflation, housing and growth forecasts. The broad story
remains one of continued economic expansion and gradually
improving labour demand, but with a more pronounced
inflation spike and slightly less momentum in consumer
spending. Our OCR call is unchanged: a pause at the October

review, followed by a 25 basis point increase to 3% at the
December Monetary Policy Statement.
A temporary inflation reprieve
We continue to think that consumer prices rose 0.7% in the
September quarter. That would lower annual inflation from
4.1% in June to 3.7% in September, broadly consistent with
the monthly price updates published so far. The final monthly
release for the quarter is due on 16 October and will help firm up
that estimate. But any easing in the annual rate is likely to prove

short-lived. We now expect prices to rise 1.1% in the December
quarter, lifting annual inflation to a three-year high of 4.2% by
year-end. That is half a percentage point above our previous
projection and above the RBNZ’s September forecast of 3.9%.
The reason is easy to see at the petrol pump. Higher global
fuel prices have been amplified by a more than 4% fall in
the New Zealand dollar since the start of September. Local
petrol prices have risen by around 50 cents a litre over the

past month, with the nationwide average price of 91 unleaded
reaching a record $3.55 a litre this week. RBNZ Governor
Breman acknowledged a couple of weeks ago that higher
oil prices have tilted the risks around the near-term inflation
outlook to the upside. Importantly, however, the RBNZ remains
focused on the medium-term inflation outlook rather than
trying to offset every movement in volatile fuel prices.
For now, we have left our quarterly inflation forecasts for 2027

unchanged. Even so, the higher starting point at the end of this
year means annual inflation is likely to remain firm through much
of 2027. Our updated profile has inflation at 3.7% in March
2027, 2.6% in June, 2.5% in September and 1.7% in December.
That is broadly similar to the RBNZ’s path through the first three
quarters, although our forecast falls more quickly late in the
year. We will take a fuller look at the 2027 inflation and growth
outlook next week alongside the monthly Market Outlook.

Housing fails to find its feet
The housing market has also delivered a disappointment. We
had expected prices to stabilise through the second half of
2026, but sales volumes have continued to weaken, and the
median time required to sell a property has lengthened to 48
days. Average prices have kept edging lower, despite firmer
conditions in parts of the South Island with greater exposure
to the strong export economy. The latest indicators do not
suggest that a decisive turn is close. We have therefore revised

our forecast for national house price growth in 2026 from a
0.6% rise to a 1.4% decline. That incorporates quarterly falls
of 0.5% in September and 0.8% in December.
The headwinds are familiar: the labour market remains soft,
mortgage rates are higher and employment growth is still
moderate. Those forces are likely to keep the market subdued
for some time, with risks tilted towards another weak year
in 2027. Changes to the taxation of capital gains on investor

housing following the 2026 General Election may add another
potential drag.
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Regional outcomes will continue to differ. Tourism- and
agriculture-focused areas should benefit from stronger income
and employment growth, supported by robust commodity export
prices. We have lifted our 2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price forecast to
$9.75/kg and set an opening forecast of $9.50/kg for 2027/28. In

contrast, homeowners in the major North Island cities may have to
wait longer for the wealth boost that comes with rising property
values. Data released this week shows that household wealth
continues to be dragged down by declining housing values.
A little less momentum
The weaker housing backdrop matters for the wider economy.
When petrol absorbs more of the household budget and home
values are drifting lower, consumers tend to become more
cautious. That combination is likely to crimp discretionary

spending in the run-up to Christmas and could take some
steam out of the construction recovery. That’s even though our
own card spending data for September  showed resilience in
consumer spending. Recent business and consumer confidence
surveys have nevertheless remained resilient despite the rise
in energy prices and long-term interest rates. Our short-term
growth forecasts therefore remain broadly intact, but we have
trimmed our December quarter consumer spending projection

and now expect GDP growth of 2.5% over 2026. That is still
a solid result, although the balance of risks has shifted to
the downside.
There are several moving parts to watch. Sustained high fuel
prices would squeeze household purchasing power more than
we have allowed for. The sharp lift in long-term interest rates
may also weigh on activity through 2027, even if shorter-
term borrowing costs evolve as expected. The election result
introduces two-sided risks that are difficult to quantify before

the shape and timing of policy changes are known. Against that,
strong agricultural incomes and signs of improving employment
growth should provide a buffer. We have not changed our 2026
labour market forecasts. Hiring appears to be strengthening
gradually, but it will take time before that improvement
translates into a noticeable fall in the unemployment rate.
The RBNZ should still pause before Christmas
Despite the higher near-term inflation track, we are not

changing our OCR forecast. Since the July Monetary Policy
Review, our view has been that the RBNZ would slow the pace
of increases as the OCR approached the Bank’s estimate of a
neutral 3%. The September meeting clearly signalled a pause
in October and one further rise in December. We continue to
regard that as the most likely path. The latest developments
raise headline inflation but, so far, there is little evidence that
inflation expectations are moving higher or that the fuel shock

is generating broader, persistent price pressure. At the same
time, growth risks warrant some caution.
A pause in October would give the Monetary Policy Committee
time to assess the cumulative effect of the increases already
delivered and to refine its strategy for 2027. Financial
conditions have also tightened from an unexpected source:
longer-term interest rates have risen markedly over the past
six weeks. That increase has at least offset the easing impulse

from the lower exchange rate, even though the weaker
currency is adding to imported inflation in the near term. We
still think the RBNZ is likely to have more work to do in 2027,
but there is plenty of data to come before that judgement
needs to be made.
For now, the best description of the outlook is resilient but
less comfortable. Export incomes remain strong, activity is
expanding and the labour market is beginning to improve. But
households face an awkward combination of record petrol

prices, soft home values and higher borrowing costs. That
mix points to a bumpy end to the year: inflation will be hotter,
spending growth a little cooler and the recovery more uneven
across regions. The RBNZ can afford to wait in October, but if
the economy develops broadly as we expect, a further hike to
3% in December will still be needed.

### Page 8

AUS: Sep Labour Force – Employment Change (000s)
Oct 15, Last: 39.5, Westpac f/c: 20 
Market f/c: 20, Range: flat to 35
Employment surprised to the upside in August with a 39k gain. 
Looking through the monthly swings, while momentum has 
improved over recent months, employment growth has largely 
continued to bump around below its historic average and the 
current pace of working age population growth.
Our preview provides a deeper look into the various gauges 
of labour demand – the LFS suggests employment growth 
is sluggish but broader measures in the Q2 Labour Account 
showed a stronger narrative. Meanwhile job ads and business 
surveys are also pulling in different directions.
For September, we have pencilled in a lift in employment of 
+20k, which would see annual growth hold in recent ranges.
AUS: Sep Labour Force – Unemployment Rate (%)
Oct 15, Last: 4.6, Westpac f/c: 4.6 
Market f/c: 4.6, Range: 4.5 to 4.7
The participation rate jumped to 67.1% in August, just under 
the 115-year high recorded in January 2025. With labour 
supply once again outstripping demand, the unemployment 
rate rose from 4.5% to 4.6% – the highest since in late-2021, 
close to rounding up to 4.7% (4.646% at three decimal places).
A softer economy and sub-par employment growth typically 
discourages some people to search for work, but cost-of-living 
pressures and interest rate rises are more than outweighing this 
effect, encouraging more people into the labour market.
With the participation rate expected to pare back to 67.0%, we 
think the unemployment rate will hold steady at 4.6%. Please 
see our preview for more detail.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.9
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Sep REINZ House Sales and Prices (%ann)
Oct 14, Sales Last: –13.0, Prices Last: –0.9
The housing market has remained soft in recent months,
with sales falling to a two-year low and prices edging lower.
Mortgage rates have risen as the RBNZ has initiated a new

tightening cycle, and a growing supply of unsold homes on the
market means that buyers are under no pressure to bid prices
higher. Conditions remain stronger in the south, with prices
up on last year in Canterbury, Otago and Southland, while
Auckland and Wellington are the weakest markets.
NZ: Sep Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Oct 16, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: 0.8
We’re forecasting a 0.8% rise in retail spending in September,
reversing the 0.9% fall seen in the previous month. In part, the

forecast rise in spending is due to the sharp increases in petrol
prices in recent weeks. Those higher fuel costs are weighing on
spending in other categories like hospitality. Nevertheless, we
still expect a 0.5% rise in core (ex-fuel) spending. Stats NZ’s
monthly retail spending figures have been very choppy for the
past two years, and we’re likely to see a bounce following last
month’s unexpected falls in categories like furnishings.
NZ: Sep Selected Consumer Prices (%mth)

Oct 16
This will be the last monthly update ahead of the full quarterly
CPI release on 22 October. We’re currently forecasting a 0.7%
quarterly rise in the CPI (+3.7%yr) and will be watching the
detail of the Selected Prices update to pin down that forecast.
In terms of the big categories, we’re forecasting a roughly 6%
rise in petrol costs, along with a 0.7% rise in food prices. That’s
balanced against continued softness in housing rents (which
are the single largest component of the CPI). The key sources

of risk for our forecast are the volatile travel categories, most
of which recorded sizeable drops last month that we expect to
partially reverse in September.

IMF ANNUAL MEETINGS
Resilience, but no relief. The IMF's latest global growth forecast is un-
changed from a year ago and from April 2026 (at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4%
in 2027), despite the energy shock, however with a different geogra -
phical breakdown: many energy importers and war-exposed countries
downgraded, and AI-exposed countries upgraded. Resilience was the

buzzword of the meetings, however it appeared mostly in the rear -
view mirror. Looking ahead, buffers are running thin, fiscal policy needs
urgent consolidation, and downside risks (from energy and AI exube -
rance) loom large. Global headline inflation is expected to rise to 4.7%
in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before easing back to 4.1% in
2027 (revised +0.2pp). Growth in advanced economies is revised up to
2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany), staying at 1.8% in 2027. Eu-

rozone growth is upgraded to 1.1% in 2026 (+0.2pp) and 1.2% in 2027.
US growth stays projected at 2.3% in 2026 and 2027 (+0.1pp). Germany
sees the largest G7 upgrade, to 1.3% in 2026 (+0.6pp) and 1.1% in 2027
(+0.1pp). France is the only major advanced economy downgraded, to
0.4% in 2026 (-0.2pp) and 0.7% in 2027 (-0.2pp). For more detailed
takeaways see Substack and this week’s EcoWeek editorial.
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Trade policy, competitiveness and budget agreements by year-end.

EU leaders asked the Commission to propose by year-end an instru -
ment allowing, in extreme cases, full closure of the single market to
a rival deemed to be attacking the EU economy. It is designed as a
“second-strike” deterrent against unfair practices and retaliation.
The Commission is also exploring extending the hybrid-vehicle safe -
guards approach agreed with China last week to other sectors such
as chemicals and machinery. On the "Made in Europe" label, vehicles

would need to be assembled in Europe with at least 70% of compo -
nent value (excluding batteries) sourced locally and with substantial
R&D in the EU. The UK would be included, provided London aligns with
EU trade defence measures. EU Council also called for decisive pro -
gress on the competitiveness agenda, with the single-market (inclu -
ding 26th regime) and simplification files to be wrapped up by year-end.
On the multi-annual financial framework, the objective remains a deal

by end-2026, so that legislation can be adopted in 2027.
EURO AREA
Moderate tightening does it. Multiple ECB Governing Council members,
speaking from the IMF Meetings in Bangkok, re-underwrote their
“moderate response” approach to the ongoing energy-driven infla -
tion surge. Accordingly, they signaled that additional rate action was
likely but not imminent, acknowledging that the rise in bond yields
was doing part of the job, and that there were no signs yet of second

round effects. Strengthening the case for moderation, the Eurozone in-
dustrial production fell in August for the second month running, by
0.5% m/m, pulled down by pharmaceutical output and motor vehicles.
That said, production in Netherlands surged to a new high (2.5% m/m
and 8.7% y/y), driven by AI machinery and equipment.
France
Business starts booming, insolvencies are stable, growth outlook is
downgraded. Business creations rose by 16% y/y in the 3 rd quarter

(+23% y/y in September), driven mainly by the digital sector and retail.
In parallel, business bankruptcies stabilised in the 3rd quarter according
to Altares, with a 0.5% increase y/y (compared with a 5.4% increase y/y
in Q2), and their impact on employment declined: 50,510 jobs at risk
in Q3, down 3% y/y (see our recent analysis). The Banque de France
halved its estimate for Q3 GDP to 0.1% q/q (same as Insee) due to
the manufacturing sector. Its monthly survey showed a sharp jump in

business uncertainty in September, driven by fuel prices and economic
deterioration (the most acute readings in industry and services since
April). The OAT-bund spread was rangebound (137-142).
Germany
Producer prices are rising. Wholesale prices rose 8.7% y/y (vs. 6.8% in
August) due to higher wholesale prices for energy products and inter -
mediate goods, particularly metals.
UNITED KINGDOM
Summer outperformance and kudos from IMF. Monthly GDP defied

expectations in August and posted a 0.1% m/m against a -0.1% consen-
sus. This marked a slowdown from July (0.4% m/m), but also a fourth
consecutive month of expansion. Industrial production underpinned
the advance: it accelerated to 0.8% m/m (consensus: -0.1%, prior: 0.3%)
thanks to pharmaceuticals. The IMF sees growth accelerating to 1.3%
in 2026 (+0.3 pp since July) and 1.2% in 2027. It also singled out the UK
among advanced economies for its efforts and progress in putting its

fiscal house in order.
UNITED STATES
Inflation heats up, manufacturing accelerates, but an October hike
is unlikely. CPI inflation exceeded expectations at 0.65% m/m in Sep -
tember (up 0.2pp), the fastest monthly pace since April. Accordingly,
the y/y rate climbed to 3.7% (up 0.3pp). The biggest upside surprise,
however, came from the core CPI: at 0.33% m/m (consensus: 0.2%, prior:
0.3%) and 2.6% y/y (consensus: 2.5%, prior: 2.4%); as services remained

sticky while goods accelerated to 0.3% m/m on autos and AI-related
items. Producer prices too rose well above expectations to 1.3% m/m
(consensus: 0.6%, prior: 0.5%) – the largest monthly increase since
March 2022 – and 6.3% y/y (consensus: 5.3%, prior: 5.6%). Industrial
production accelerated in September to 0.7% m/m (up 0.6pp) thanks
to utilities. Manufacturing returned to growth (0.3% m/m, up 0.5pp)
with gains broadening beyond the AI-related and aerospace sectors.

Retail sales excl. autos and gasoline, by contrast, slowed sharply to
0.2% m/m (down 0.9pp). During his speech at the IMF Meetings after
the hotter than expected inflation prints, Chair Warsh declined to hint
at an imminent rate hike. We continue to expect the next rate hike to
occur in December.
To watch this week:
- Developed markets Flash PMIs for October (Friday).
- Eurozone Germany September PPI, October ZEW (Tuesday), France Insee Business Climate (Thursday).

- UK August Employment data (Tuesday), September CPI and PPI (Wednesday), September Retail Sales (Friday).
- Japan September CPI, October PMI (Friday).

for a changing

Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
JAPAN
Producer price inflation eases. Producer prices fell for a second month
in a row in September, down a further 0.5% m/m, while the yearly rate
slowed for the first time since February (-0.8pp), though it remains
firm at 6.8%. The IMF forecasts growth at 0.7% in both 2026 and 2027,
broadly unchanged. The yen weakened past 158 per dollar again after

reports that the BoJ is leaning against a back-to-back October rate hike.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Emerging markets
Asia’s outlook upgraded, other regions downgraded.
The IMF upgraded its Asia growth forecast to 4.8% in 2026 and 4.4% in
2027. The largest upgrades are for economies well integrated into the
AI supply chain, notably Taiwan (+5.5pp to 10.7%), South Korea (+0.7pp
to 3.3%), Singapore, Vietnam, Malaysia and to some extent Thailand.
India is forecast to grow 7% in both 2026 and 2027 (fiscal year basis),

up 0.6 and 0.3pp from July on robust domestic demand.
Central Europe’s GDP growth is projected at 2.1% in 2026, slightly
lower than April’s forecast. In 2027, it remains unchanged at 2.3%.
Growth paths are diverse within the region.
In the Middle East & North Africa, GDP is expected to contract by 2%
in 2026. GCC real GDP should fall by 4.8% with only Oman expected
to post positive growth (4.1%). A marked rebound is expected for 2027
(MENA: 7%; GCC: 9.1%).

The growth outlook for Latin America remains subdued, revised down
to 2.3% in 2026 (from 2.4%) and 2.5% in 2027 (from 2.7%). However, in
its "International Trade Outlook 2026" published on 15 October, the
Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) ex-
pects the region to experience a trade boom in 2026: goods exports are
projected to surge by 20% in value terms (11% from price increases and
9% from volume growth), with trade to Asia (excluding China) soaring

by 32%, while intraregional trade growth lags behind at 8%. Yet, these
export gains are concentrated in commodities and low-value-added
manufacturing, limiting their spillover to broader growth. Stronger
trade is thus providing only a modest boost to the economy.
China
Explosive export growth, subdued inflation and credit growth. In Sep-
tember, export value growth surged to 36.3% y/y from 25% in August,
driven by semiconductors, computer equipment and other AI goods, as

well as green technologies. Export prices surged, especially for semi -
conductors, while total export volumes remained robust. Import value
growth also accelerated to 31% y/y from 28.2% in August, boosted by
both rising volume and rapidly increasing prices. The trade surplus
hit a new monthly record of USD 134 bn in September. CPI inflation
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, mainly because of a 14.9% rise in
vehicle fuel prices. Food prices continued to fall (-1.7% y/y) and core

inflation was stable at 1%. Producer price inflation accelerated to 5%
(the highest since 2022) due to rising commodity prices. Growth in to-
tal domestic credit outstanding (“social financing”) continued to slow
down, reaching a record low of 7.1% y/y in September. Bank loans to
households declined (-2% y/y) for the seventh consecutive month.
In Q3 2026, real GDP growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y (1.2% q/q) from
4.3% y/y (0.9% q/q), primarily led by industrial production (which rose by

5.1% y/y on average in Q3 vs. 4.6% in Q2). On the demand side, exports
were the key driver while fixed-asset investment continued to contract.
Retail sales posted a small pickup in September but remained flat
in real terms in Q3 as a whole. With growth reaching 4.6% y/y year-to-
date, China needs only a 4.2% growth in Q4 to meet the official target of
4.5%-5% for this year – which seems easily feasible. The IMF trimmed
its growth forecast by 0.1pp to 4.5% in 2026 and 4% in 2027.

Indonesia
MSCI launched a consultation on reclassifying the country from Emer-
ging Market to Standalone Market status, with a final decision due by
end-June 2027, citing opaque shareholding structures and suspected
coordinating trading. Since January 2026, MSCI had frozen Index ad -
justments and threatened only a downgrade to Frontier market status
(like Pakistan), but market participants argued that a Frontier reclas -
sification would not properly reflect the extent of the market’s pro -

blems. Over the medium term, the risk is heavy foreign capital outflows
at a time when net FDI no longer covers the current account deficit.
This leaves Indonesia reliant on foreign portfolio inflows and the ru -
piah exposed to a reversal in sentiment. On the day of this announce -
ment, the Jakarta Composite Index fell by 11.6%, its steepest drop since
January 1998. Among Asian emerging markets, Indonesia has posted
the worst stock market performance since the beginning of the year

(-40.7%).
COMMODITIES
Iran/Oil. More of the same. Brent oil prices have remained broadly
stable this week (0.5% at 107$/b on Monday). Renewed Iranian at -
tacks on tankers in the Strait and intensification of Houthis’ attack over
the Saudi territory feed the geopolitical risk premium on barrel prices.
But IEA announcement of an accelerated 100 mb oil release (above
the March initial target of 400 mb) and reports that the US may lift
sanctions on the Russian and Iranian shadow fleets have contributed to

crude price stability. The spread with physical barrel (Dated Brent) has
declined (6.7$/b on Oct 16th). Trump administration’s various attempts
to lower diesel prices have had a limited impact for now. European gas
prices (TTF) recorded another weekly rise (4.7% w/w to 88.4 €/MWh,
the highest level since Dec 22nd, 2022) on the back of the rising number
of attacks on vessels in the Gulf and persisting disruption in the Strait.
However, EU gas inventories reached 74% of full capacities on October

16th (i.e., almost at the 80% month-end target), which should reduce
support for TTF prices going forward.
y/y = year-on-year
m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels

Document 305: FX Daily_ Dollar has lost its safe-haven crown

Report source: ING Think

### Page 1

The yen and Swiss franc have emerged as the safe havens of
choice as the greenback continues to show some vulnerability
to the AI story. A further widening in French spreads has not
materially hit the euro, but that correlation may spike again
soon. Today, PMIs are published, but focus will remain primarily
on global equities, France and oil

Frantisek Taborsky, Francesco Pesole and Chris Turner
The dollar’s vulnerability to the AI story is driving safe-haven demand
elsewhere, notably towards the yen and Swiss franc
USD: Losing safe-haven appeal
Yesterday was a rough session for risk sentiment, as global equities
sold oﬀ on a combination of higher oil prices and AI-related
concerns. FX moves conﬁrmed that the dollar is far from the
preferred safe haven at this stage, with the yen and Swiss franc
emerging as the main beneﬁciaries of the defensive rotation.
Crucially, the negative correlation between bonds and equities is
reasserting itself, and FX markets are taking their cues from that
dynamic. 10yr Treasuries broke back below 5.0% for the ﬁrst time

in a month, showing ever-reduced sensitivity to higher oil prices.
The same dynamic is visible at the front end, where the 2yr SOFR
swap rate dropped 15bp to 4.50% yesterday. That suggests equity
jitters are enough to dent market conviction on a large Federal
Reserve tightening cycle.
Still, the bar for pricing out a December Fed hike appears fairly high,
which should prevent larger USD corrections for the moment. Any
stabilisation in risk sentiment can help USD consolidate across

most pairs and take some shine oﬀ JPY and CHF. But the FX reaction
function to further risk sell-oﬀs is now looking much clearer.
On the data side, focus will be on S&P Global PMIs today.
Consensus is for some softening in the US ﬁgures, which, however,
remain deep into expansionary territory.
Francesco Pesole
EUR: Sensitivity to France can return
rapidly
The OAT-Bund 10yr spread closed at 174bp after touching 178bp
yesterday. That wasn’t the only EU sovereign spread widening

yesterday; bunds outperformed (+9bp) on haven demand, and
energy prices played a role too.
But OATs saw the only increase in 10yr yields in Europe (+6bp), and
the OAT-BTP spread has reached a grim 50bp milestone. The
budget/political situation is not visibly deteriorating, so the move is
probably a function of OAT’s simply having a higher beta to global
factors and perhaps some concerns about another weekend of
social unrest in France.
The euro’s sensitivity to France has clearly diminished, with the

common currency not materially underperforming G10 peers
yesterday. That link may, however, re-establish very rapidly should
a new pain level – perhaps 200bp – be breached. We aren’t
expecting that much further widening for now, but the risks are
clearly there, and the euro remains rather unattractive across the
board.

### Page 3

EUR/CHF remains the cleanest way to play European sovereign risk
at the moment, while EUR/USD should be quick to re-test the 1.1120
14 October low should bond turmoil persist. Today’s PMIs will
probably play a secondary role to France, which faces a Moody’s
credit review, and global equities. Expectations are for slightly softer
numbers compared to September.
Francesco Pesole
CEE: Global risk-off sentiment weighs on
region currencies
The end of the week oﬀers little in the regional calendar, with the

primary driver remaining the broader global narrative. Deepening
"risk-oﬀ" sentiment and the continued rise in energy prices are
weighing on emerging market currencies, including those in CEE.
Nevertheless, the region is demonstrating admirable resilience;
despite some ﬂuctuations in rates, the FX market remains relatively
robust. Even so, we maintain a bearish outlook on CEE currencies in
general. EUR/CZK and EUR/RON have reached the targets we
discussed here on Monday.

In the Czech Republic, we saw an interview yesterday with Czech
National Bank Deputy Governor Jan Frait, marking the start of the
current round of oﬃcial commentary ahead of the November
policy meeting. Although the market interpreted the interview as
dovish, we view it as preparation for a rate hike. Furthermore, as
Frait is one of the more dovish members of the Bank Board,
subsequent comments from other oﬃcials are likely to be more
hawkish. This supports further strengthening of the koruna, despite

a signiﬁcantly negative global environment; consequently, we
could see a test of the 24.200–24.250 range.
EUR/RON has normalised, returning to the 5.340 level, following the
parliament’s vote of conﬁdence in the new technocratic
government. As we noted previously, we do not expect the new
government to trigger an FX rally, but rather to foster stabilisation
around current levels. This remains our baseline view, though the
risk remains tilted to the upside for EUR/RON should political

uncertainty return.

US Monetary Policy FX Eurozone
Emerging markets
On the bearish side, the Polish zloty and the Hungarian forint
remain particularly vulnerable. They may face further downside,
given the absence of a hawkish stance from their respective central
banks, combined with high exposure to energy imports and rising
energy prices. The upside for EUR/HUF should be capped in the 368–
370 range, supported by a local, idiosyncratic narrative; we would
expect to see new sellers of EUR/HUF emerge there. Conversely,

EUR/PLN lacks this beneﬁt and remains exposed to the upside.
Frantisek Taborsky
MXN: Banxico has done a number on the
peso
Since US rates exploded higher at the start of September, the
Mexican peso is by far the worst-performing EM currency. It is close
to 8% weaker versus the dollar. The next worst currency is the
Romanian leu at around 4%. The peso has been hit hard by dovish
Banxico rhetoric, outlining that it did not plan to mechanically track

the Fed with rate hikes. Should Banxico fail to match a presumed
rate hike from the Fed in December, the Banxico-Fed policy rate
spread will reach the narrowest on record and leave the peso
exposed at a diﬃcult time for risk assets.
Barring a hawkish surprise from Banxico at its 5 November
meeting, we expect the peso to remain vulnerable. We note that in
the IMF ﬁnancial stability report, Mexico was seen to have the near
entirety of its foreign portfolio investments in US securities, which

the IMF saw as AI-chasing behaviour. Unrest in the tech sector may
therefore be weighing on the peso. 19.00/19.10 looks to be the
trajectory for USD/MXN over the coming weeks.
Chris Turner

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
BULLETIN
Persistent energy pressures have further to run
19 October 2026
Key points
• Shipping disruptions, depleted
inventories and elevated freight
costs are keeping physical crude
and fuel markets tight despite a

reported recovery in Gulf exports.
• Brent is forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing
to US$77/bbl in Q4 2027.
• Refined product markets are
expected to ease more slowly than
crude, with elevated gasoil cracks,
shipping and insurance costs slowing
declines in petrol and diesel prices.
• Under an escalation scenario,
renewed conflict and deeper supply
disruptions could push Brent towards
US$150/bbl in H1 2027, prolonging
elevated fuel and LNG prices.

• Under an early resolution scenario, a
durable US-Iran agreement accelerates
supply recovery, taking Brent towards
US$65/bbl by Q4 2027 and bringing
fuel and LNG prices down faster.
Q4 2026 forecasts:
Brent crude
US$101/bbl
Japanese LNG
US$24.5/MMBtu

### Page 2

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should

ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could
push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
This article was first published in the October Market Outlook .
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating

global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite reported crude exports through the Strait of
Hormuz recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels
in late September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back to
normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings have
risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving visible

transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo transfers in
the Gulf of Oman have increased as an alternative route.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly. Ship-
to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts through
the Gulf via a route close to the Oman shoreline cause delays
while some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships are
also available, with many redeployed to other markets. Higher
insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-China

tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher than a
year ago. With prompt barrels (available for physical delivery)
scarce, the gap between physical and futures prices widened
again, with Dated Brent around US$136/bbl in early October
against US$101/bbl for front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes

on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the
Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened
to an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October,
from US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the Australian
government’s excise tax returning to normal, after it had been

halved, this has lifted national diesel and petrol prices to
around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a
return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average

around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around
US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of

this reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist

### Page 3

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
attacks on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below
its pre-conflict level until H2 2027. Meanwhile, we look for UAE
production to continue to improve over 2027 and lift above
pre-conflict levels from 2028, as it is no longer constrained by

OPEC quotas. We do not expect a meaningful return of Iranian
oil until an agreement is reached, and damage sustained during
the conflict means production will take time to recover.
We expect global oil demand to be 103.7mb/d in 2026, 0.8mb/d
(0.7%) higher than in 2025, and to rise a further 1.9mb/d (1.8%)
to 105.6mb/d in 2027. Chinese crude imports will continue to
recover over the coming months as refiners restock ahead of
Lunar New Year, but we expect only a gradual return to pre-

conflict levels, as the electrification of transport limits the
recovery in Chinese demand. Globally, demand for diesel and jet
fuel recovers as transport and aviation activity picks up, although
some demand destruction and efficiency gains are expected
to be permanent. The conflict has also highlighted strategic
vulnerabilities, particularly across Asia, so we expect a push to
rebuild reserves adding around 0.5% to demand over 2027.
Fuel prices to ease more slowly than crude

Refined product supply is expected to recover more slowly
than crude, with Gulf refinery runs not returning to pre-war
levels until at least late 2027. Outside the Gulf, there has
been some constructive news. The US has lifted sanctions
on Russian diesel until April 2027, with Russia saying it will
supply more than 4Mt over the coming months, while China
has lifted its ban on refined product exports. However, we
are cautious about how much this will add to the market in

the short term. Much of the Russian supply is headed to the
US and will depend on Russia’s ability to restore refining and
export capacity, while doubts remain over the Russia-Ukraine
energy ceasefire announced by President Trump. China still
manages exports through quotas, which are likely to increase
only gradually as domestic stocks are rebuilt. Refinery runs
should lift ahead of Lunar New Year, but any increase in
exports will take time to reach regional markets.

With product supply recovering slowly and depleted inventories
being rebuilt, Singapore gasoil crack is likely to stay above its
long-run average even as Brent eases, narrowing from US$71/
bbl in Q4 to US$27/bbl by Q4 2027. With Singapore Gas 10ppm
prices, shipping and insurance costs to remain elevated and
easing only gradually in 2027, we forecast Australian diesel
to retail at an average of 274¢/L in Q4, easing to 200¢/L in Q4
2027, and petrol at 230¢/L and 187¢/L respectively.

LNG prices supported through 2027
Japanese LNG prices surged 15.2%mth in September,
averaging US$25.4/MMBtu as supply disruptions in Qatar
and its inability to divert exports around the Strait of Hormuz
tightened markets and intensified competition between
importers. While prices are now more than double pre-conflict
levels, demand destruction across Asia is providing some
offset to tight supply
Our Q4 forecast for Japanese LNG is now US$24.5/mmbtu and

we expect the market to remain structurally tight, with supply
recovery likely to be gradual and prices not returning to pre-
conflict levels until around 2028. Still, Australia’s domestic
gas market should remain insulated, supported by the FY2027
Federal Budget’s domestic gas reservation scheme, which
requires exporters to supply the domestic market with the
equivalent of up to 20% of gas exports. So far this year,
despite the increase in LNG spot prices in Asia, Australian

natural gas prices have remained under the $12 per GJ cap
that has proposed by the Commonwealth government. We are
expecting this to continue well into 2027.
Risks around our oil price outlook are two-sided
Talks are still ongoing in the background, with the US and
Iran exchanging proposals to reopen the Strait, but the gap
between them remains wide. The conflict has also expanded
to the Red Sea and, with inventories sharply drawn down,
there is a smaller buffer to absorb a further shock.

Using the Oxford Economics model, we assess an escalation
scenario, where energy markets face a deeper and more
persistent disruption as the conflict in the Middle East
intensifies. In this case, Brent futures rise to around US$150/
bbl in H1 2027, only dipping to below US$100/bbl in Q2 2028.
We also consider an early resolution scenario, where shipping
and Gulf supply normalise faster, returning Brent to near pre-
conflict levels by end-2027.
Escalation scenario

In the escalation scenario we assume:
Talks stall and the conflict escalates:  the gap between the
US and Iran proves too wide and the US resumes strikes after
the midterms. Iran responds with attacks on shipping and Gulf
energy infrastructure, damaging export, storage and refining
facilities. A lasting agreement is not reached until 2028.
Export routes are disrupted: more frequent attacks curtail
dark crossings of the Strait and ship-to-ship transfers in the

Gulf of Oman. Houthi attacks also cut off Saudi alternative
routes, forcing Gulf producers to cut output and Middle
East crude exports sharply lower. New routes and shipping
arrangements eventually support a recovery in Gulf flows, but
trade patterns take time to adjust.
... and risks remain and while they are two sided ...

4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Product supply fails to recover:  The Russia–Ukraine energy
ceasefire does not hold and strikes on Russian refineries
continue, limiting how much diesel Russia can export,
even with the US sanctions waiver extended. China also

reintroduces its export ban to protect domestic supply.
Oil prices lift to around US$150/bbl in H1 2027: With little
buffer to absorb the supply loss, competition for physical
delivery intensifies, with physical prices well above futures
as prompt barrels remain scarce. Higher prices support
supply growth outside OPEC and drive demand destruction,
alongside further emergency stock releases, but a deeper
and more prolonged global deficit remains. Brent stays above

US$100/bbl until mid-2028 as governments rebuild reserves.
The Singapore gasoil crack widens and, with freight and
insurance costs also higher, Australian diesel and petrol prices
average around 247 cpl and 231 cpl at end 2027. Japanese
LNG also remains elevated around US$23.2/MMBtu in Q4
2027, $9.1 above our baseline.
Early resolution scenario
In the early resolution scenario we assume:
A durable agreement: The US and Iran agree terms to reopen
the Strait by the end of year, and, unlike the June interim deal,

the agreement holds, with progress towards a permanent
settlement in early 2027. Attacks on shipping cease and the
US blockade is lifted, allowing Iranian oil back into the market,
initially from storage. Vessels are quickly repositioned to the
Gulf, with freight and war-risk insurance easing from early
2027 rather than mid-year.
A faster recovery in supply:  With export routes secure, Gulf
production and damaged refinery and export capacity recover

sooner than in the baseline. Outside the Gulf, the US extends
its waiver on Russian diesel beyond April 2027 and China
raises export quotas as domestic stocks are rebuilt.
Demand settles below pre-conflict trend: Some demand
destruction and efficiency gains persist, particularly in China.
With inventories rebuilding faster, government restocking is
smaller and more gradual than in the baseline.
Prices fall quickly, but restocking provides a floor:  Brent

Brent futures ease to around US$88/bbl in Q1 2027 as the
premium for prompt physical barrels unwinds. As the market
moves into balance, Brent eases to around US$65/bbl by Q4
2027, close to pre-conflict levels.
Refined product prices ease faster on lower freight costs with
the Singapore gasoil crack narrowing. Australian diesel and
petrol prices fall to around 182 cpl and 170 cpl by Q4 2027. Gas
markets also ease, although damage to Qatar’s gas facility means

export capacity recovers gradually. Japanese LNG falls to around
US$11.9/MMBtu by end-2027, $2.1 below our baseline.
Economic impact
In the escalation scenario, higher fuel costs flow through to
transport, food and other energy-intensive goods such as
plastics and fertilisers. Australian headline CPI remains above
4.5% for much of 2027, with underlying inflation remaining
sticky at above 3% until late 2028. The RBA faces a difficult
trade off between much higher inflation and a significant

hit to activity, as households cut discretionary spending and
businesses defer investment. Higher LNG export earnings
provide only a moderate offset. In the early resolution scenario,
headline inflation is around 0.1ppt below the baseline in 2027.
New Zealand is more exposed in both scenarios, with food
and energy a larger share of its CPI and no offsetting energy
exports. Headline inflation is around 1ppt higher in the
escalation scenario in Q1 2027.

... the upside presents a more dramatic shift in prices
